---
# Joe Biden — United States, 2023

## 1. Overview

Joe Biden was President of the United States throughout 2023, exercising executive authority over foreign policy, federal administration, the armed forces, regulation, and enforcement. His influence was substantial but constrained by Congress, federal courts, state governments, independent agencies, treaty partners, and a highly polarized electorate. Republicans controlled the House of Representatives; Democrats held the Senate, making major legislation dependent on cross-party negotiation.

The period combined strong employment and investment outcomes with persistent inflation, intense immigration and border disputes, continued support for Ukraine, the Gaza war after October 7, and congressional investigations into Biden-family business dealings and classified documents. Information quality was relatively high compared with many countries, but partisan polarization, litigation, secrecy around national-security matters, fragmented crime data, and competing political narratives complicated attribution.

## 2. Evidence records

### E001 — Executive authority was constrained by divided government

Congress enacted the Fiscal Responsibility Act of 2023 on June 3. The law suspended the debt limit through January 1, 2025, imposed discretionary-spending caps, rescinded some unobligated funds, changed work requirements, and ended the pause on student-loan payments. This demonstrates both Biden’s ability to secure a consequential compromise and the limits imposed by a Republican House and closely divided Senate.

Strongest source: Congressional Research Service, “Debt Limit Suspensions,” updated 2024, and Congress.gov bill summary. The relevant sections describe the June 3 enactment, suspension period, spending caps, and program changes. [CRS debt-limit summary](https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/IN11829), [Congress.gov H.R. 3746 summary](https://www.congress.gov/bill/118th-congress/house-bill/3746/summary/00)

Period: 2023.

Connection to Biden: strong. The president signed the legislation, but the outcome was negotiated with congressional leaders and reflected Congress’s constitutional control over borrowing and appropriations.

Complications: The agreement avoided an immediate default but accepted spending constraints and policy concessions. The administration did not control the underlying debt-limit mechanism.

Cautions: CRS is authoritative but summarizes legislation rather than evaluating political responsibility. Congress.gov provides legal status and bill content, not an impartial account of bargaining.

Independent support: CRS analyses of the Fiscal Responsibility Act and debt-limit episode.

### E002 — Continued support for Ukraine increased U.S. strategic involvement and escalation risk

In 2023 the Biden administration continued military, economic, and humanitarian support for Ukraine and coordinated with a broad coalition of allies. The policy aimed to help Ukraine resist Russia’s invasion while avoiding direct U.S.–Russia war. U.S. support carried risks of escalation, weapons-stockpile pressure, and domestic political opposition.

Strongest source: U.S. Department of State, “What does unity look like?,” describing allied and U.S. support and humanitarian consequences of the war, including the destruction of the Kakhovka dam. [Department of State Ukraine coalition feature](https://stories.state.gov/g7-ukraine/index.html)

Period: 2023, with the war itself inherited from 2022.

Connection to Biden: strong for U.S. aid and alliance policy; weaker for battlefield outcomes, which depended heavily on Ukrainian decisions, Russian actions, and allied contributions.

Complications: Support strengthened Ukraine’s position and transatlantic coordination, but did not end the war in 2023. The policy also faced concerns about escalation and finite military supplies.

Cautions: State Department material is an official advocacy and information product and may emphasize coalition success over costs or disagreement.

Independent support: CRS and Carnegie Endowment analyses of the administration’s Ukraine policy; State Department travel and security advisories documenting continuing combat risks.

### E003 — The October 7 attacks and Gaza war created a major foreign-policy crisis

After Hamas’s October 7 attack on Israel, Biden rapidly supported Israel, sought the release of hostages, pressed for humanitarian assistance, and engaged regional governments. The administration’s support for Israel became a source of domestic and international controversy as civilian deaths and humanitarian conditions in Gaza worsened.

Strongest source: U.S. Department of State travel and diplomatic records, including the Secretary of State’s regional travel record for October 2023 and the State Department’s Middle East statements. The State Department historian records Biden’s October 2023 meetings in Israel, Jordan, Bahrain, Qatar, the UAE, and Saudi Arabia. [State Department travel record](https://history.state.gov/departmenthistory/travels/secretary/blinken-antony-j)

Period: 2023.

Connection to Biden: strong for diplomatic posture, military assistance, and humanitarian policy; indirect for Israeli military conduct, which was undertaken by a separate sovereign government.

Complications: U.S. pressure contributed to humanitarian diplomacy and aid efforts, but did not prevent extensive civilian harm or resolve the conflict.

Cautions: Official diplomatic records establish actions and meetings but are less useful for measuring civilian impact or opposition perspectives.

Independent support: Associated Press reporting on U.S. policy, Israeli strikes, humanitarian conditions, and Biden’s refusal to request an immediate cease-fire; UN Security Council Resolution 2720.

### E004 — Inflation slowed while employment remained strong, but living-cost pressure persisted

BLS reported that the annual average unemployment rate was 3.6% in 2023. Consumer prices rose 4.1% on average from 2022 to 2023, a major deceleration from 8.0% the previous year but still a substantial loss of purchasing power. Employment and wage growth were strong, but public perceptions remained negative because prices stayed above their pre-inflation baseline.

Strongest sources: U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, “Regional and State Unemployment—2023 Annual Averages,” and “Consumer Expenditures in 2023.” [BLS unemployment report](https://www.bls.gov/news.release/archives/srgune_03012024.htm), [BLS consumer expenditures report](https://www.bls.gov/opub/reports/consumer-expenditures)

Period: 2023.

Connection to Biden: partial. Federal fiscal policy, investment legislation, energy policy, and regulatory choices affected conditions, but inflation was also shaped by post-pandemic demand, supply disruptions, global energy markets, and Federal Reserve monetary policy, which is institutionally independent.

Complications: The labor-market result was favorable, but inflation remained politically damaging. Nominal GDP or current-dollar consumption should not be treated as real growth without adjustment.

Cautions: BLS statistics are high quality but describe national outcomes rather than presidential responsibility.

Independent support: Federal Reserve releases, Congressional Budget Office analyses, and AP economic reporting.

### E005 — Biden’s industrial and climate program moved into implementation

The administration implemented the Inflation Reduction Act and infrastructure legislation through grants, tax incentives, clean-energy programs, semiconductor investment, and public works. The policy sought to increase domestic production, reduce emissions, create jobs, and improve resilience. Its benefits were distributed over multiple years and depended on private investment and state-level implementation.

Strongest source: Congressional Research Service, “The Biden Administration’s Climate Policies,” which reviews the administration’s climate laws, regulations, and implementation. [CRS climate-policy report](https://crsreports.congress.gov/product/pdf/R/R47068)

Period: 2023 implementation of legislation enacted mainly in 2021–2022.

Connection to Biden: strong for legislative agenda, executive implementation, and administrative priorities; weaker for realized long-term emissions and employment effects.

Complications: The program generated major investment but also drew criticism over subsidies, permitting, fiscal cost, and benefits to corporations. Some results were not yet measurable in 2023.

Cautions: CRS provides policy analysis, not a final causal evaluation. Partisan congressional reports should be used as critiques rather than neutral evidence.

Independent support: Associated Press analysis of “Bidenomics,” academic studies of Inflation Reduction Act effects, and Congressional Budget Office materials.

### E006 — Immigration policy combined restrictive enforcement with humanitarian pathways

The administration continued to manage high migration and asylum pressure at the southern border. It used parole and humanitarian programs while also relying on restrictive enforcement measures and asylum rules. The policy produced competing claims: supporters emphasized order and humanitarian alternatives; critics argued that restrictions could violate international protection obligations.

Strongest source: Congressional Research Service, “The Biden Administration’s Immigration Policies.” [CRS immigration-policy report](https://crsreports.congress.gov/product/pdf/R/R47501)

Period: 2023, with migration pressures partly inherited from earlier years.

Connection to Biden: strong for federal border policy, executive rules, and DHS implementation; limited by court rulings, congressional inaction, state governments, and conditions in origin and transit countries.

Complications: The same policy could reduce unauthorized crossings in some contexts while restricting access to asylum and increasing risks for migrants. Attribution is especially difficult because border outcomes depend on foreign governments and migration networks.

Cautions: CRS PDF access was technically unreliable during inspection; the catalogue also included AP and Leadership Conference materials that require separate critical reading.

Independent support: Associated Press reporting, DHS statistics, UNHCR materials, and litigation records.

### E007 — The administration pursued new AI safeguards through executive action

On October 30, 2023, Biden issued an executive-order initiative on safe, secure, and trustworthy artificial intelligence. The accompanying OMB analysis described directives concerning safety, civil rights, innovation, competition, workers, consumers, privacy, and U.S. leadership. The order relied primarily on executive agencies and therefore depended on administrative capacity and future implementation.

Strongest source: Office of Management and Budget, “Budgetary Impact Analysis for Executive Order Entitled ‘Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence,’” October 30, 2023. [OMB analysis](https://whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/AI-EO-BIS.pdf)

Period: 2023.

Connection to Biden: strong for executive agenda-setting; long-term effects were not yet established.

Complications: Executive action could be implemented faster than legislation but was vulnerable to later administrative reversal and legal or resource constraints.

Cautions: The OMB document describes intended benefits and reports no budgetary impact; it is not an independent evaluation of effectiveness.

Independent support: Federal Register text of Executive Order 14110, the Carnegie Endowment’s foreign-policy and technology analyses, and academic AI-governance research.

### E008 — Federal crime data showed improvement in homicide but persistent unequal risks

The Bureau of Justice Statistics reported a 2023 homicide-victimization rate of 5.9 per 100,000 people, down from 6.7 in 2022. The rate for Black Americans was 21.3 per 100,000, compared with 3.2 for white Americans. These data indicate improvement in the national homicide rate alongside severe racial inequality.

Strongest source: Bureau of Justice Statistics, “Homicide Victimization in the United States, 2023,” published May 2025, based on FBI NIBRS and Supplementary Homicide Reports. [BJS homicide report](https://bjs.ojp.gov/library/publications/homicide-victimization-united-states-2023)

Period: 2023; published later as retrospective evidence.

Connection to Biden: limited to moderate. Federal grants, firearms policy, policing standards, and Justice Department enforcement matter, but most policing and public-safety authority lies with state and local governments.

Complications: National crime trends cannot be attributed directly to the president. The decline in homicide coexisted with substantial disparities.

Cautions: The report was published after the requested period and was updated after initial release. Crime reporting coverage and definitions have changed, complicating year-to-year comparison.

Independent support: FBI crime data, CDC mortality statistics, and academic studies of 2023 homicide trends.

### E009 — Classified-document handling raised accountability questions but did not produce criminal charges

Later retrospective evidence from Special Counsel Robert Hur’s February 2024 report concerned classified documents found at the Penn Biden Center and Biden’s Delaware residence. The investigation examined retention and disclosure of classified materials and concluded that criminal charges were not warranted, while criticizing aspects of document handling and discussing evidentiary and intent issues.

Strongest source: U.S. Department of Justice, Special Counsel Robert K. Hur, “Report on the Investigation Into Unauthorized Removal, Retention, and Disclosure of Classified Documents,” February 5, 2024. [DOJ special-counsel page](https://www.justice.gov/archives/sco-hur)

Period: later retrospective finding concerning conduct before and during the presidency.

Connection to Biden: strong for personal accountability and document-handling procedures; the report did not establish a criminal offense warranting prosecution.

Complications: The investigation’s conclusion differed politically from the criminal prosecution of Donald Trump in a separate classified-documents case. Biden’s lawyers disputed parts of Hur’s characterization.

Cautions: This is later evidence, not a 2023 contemporaneous record. The report was produced by a special counsel appointed by the attorney general and became part of a highly polarized political debate.

Independent support: DOJ appointment and closure records, National Archives materials, congressional investigations, and contemporaneous AP reporting.

## 3. Information environment and limitations

The United States had extensive official statistics, court records, legislative documents, inspector-general processes, congressional investigations, independent media, and academic research. Information access was therefore comparatively strong.

Important limitations remained:

- National-security and diplomatic decisions were partly classified.
- Federal crime data were affected by reporting coverage, changing systems, and state/local variation.
- Presidential responsibility was difficult to separate from Congress, the Federal Reserve, courts, states, foreign governments, and private actors.
- Partisan polarization produced sharply conflicting interpretations of Biden-family investigations, immigration, Israel/Gaza, Ukraine, inflation, and election administration.
- Official administration sources were useful for documenting decisions but often emphasized intended benefits.
- AP, CRS, BLS, BJS, DOJ, and State Department materials were generally stronger than partisan congressional reports or opinion pieces, but each has a defined institutional perspective.
- The 2023 period contains inherited conditions—post-pandemic inflation, Russia’s invasion, migration pressures, and existing document-handling events—that should not be treated as wholly created by Biden.

## 4. Questions for deeper research

1. What measurable military, humanitarian, and diplomatic effects did U.S. support produce in Ukraine during 2023?
2. How did U.S. arms transfers and diplomatic pressure affect civilian protection and humanitarian access in Gaza?
3. What portion of 2023 inflation and employment outcomes can plausibly be attributed to Biden-era fiscal and industrial policy?
4. Did 2023 immigration rules improve border management without unlawfully restricting asylum?
5. What concrete implementation results followed the AI executive order by the end of 2023?
6. How did federal grants and Justice Department policy affect state and local homicide trends?
7. What did congressional investigations establish, as opposed to merely allege, about Biden-family business dealings?
8. How effective were Biden’s stated goals on climate, infrastructure, labor, health costs, and social welfare by the end of the year?

## 5. Corroborating sources opened and useful mainly for support

- Congressional Research Service, *The Biden Administration’s Foreign Policy*.
- Congressional Research Service, *The Biden Administration’s Climate Policies*.
- Congressional Research Service, *The Biden Administration’s Immigration Policies*.
- Associated Press reporting on Ukraine, Gaza, Bidenomics, asylum, student loans, and Biden-family investigations.
- U.S. Department of State records on Ukraine and the Middle East.
- Bureau of Labor Statistics 2023 employment, inflation, and expenditure releases.
- GAO, *Student Loans: Education Should Proactively Manage Fraud Risks in Any Future Debt Relief Efforts*, describing the Supreme Court’s 2023 invalidation of Biden’s broad debt-relief program. [GAO report](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-24-107142)
- BJS and FBI crime-statistics materials.
- Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights timeline and progress report.

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full Federal Register text and implementation records for Executive Order 14110.
- CRS immigration report R47501, including its legal analysis of asylum restrictions.
- UN Security Council Resolution 2720 and humanitarian implementation records.
- Congressional Oversight and Judiciary Committee records on the Biden-family investigations.
- Full Hur report with page-level extraction and responses from Biden’s counsel.
- CBO and Treasury analyses of the Fiscal Responsibility Act’s fiscal effects.
- Independent evaluations of Inflation Reduction Act implementation and emissions outcomes.
- Freedom House, V-Dem, RSF, and Transparency International records for 2023, used only as national context rather than direct presidential attribution.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Chapter 1B — Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility  
Joe Biden, United States, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Joe Biden was President and commander-in-chief throughout 2023. He had primary executive authority over nuclear policy, the Department of Defense, State Department arms-control diplomacy, intelligence, export controls, and federal AI and biosecurity policy. His authority was constrained by Congress’s control over appropriations and statutory authorization, Senate treaty powers, military and intelligence institutions, allied governments, Russia’s cooperation, courts, and the technical complexity of nuclear systems.

The inherited baseline was a large U.S. nuclear arsenal, an ongoing modernization program, deteriorating U.S.–Russia relations, Russia’s war against Ukraine, rapid Chinese nuclear expansion, and a weakened arms-control architecture. Biden’s administration extended New START at the beginning of his presidency, but Russia suspended participation in February 2023 and had already stopped facilitating inspections. During 2023, the administration continued deterrence and modernization while publicly supporting arms control, nonproliferation, crisis communication, and new norms for AI and other emerging technologies.

The strongest evidence is available for formal policy, treaty status, official statements, and appointments. The most difficult issues are classified command-and-control arrangements, actual readiness, the effectiveness of nuclear safeguards, the long-term impact of modernization, and the degree to which U.S. support for Ukraine increased or reduced escalation risk.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Evidence status |
|---|---|
| 1B.1 Reducing existential risk | Evidence supports active risk-reduction diplomacy and safeguards, but also continued modernization and deterrence |
| 1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric | Evidence supports generally restrained U.S. rhetoric; public messaging was firm during Russia’s nuclear signaling |
| 1B.3 Safe nuclear control | Formal institutional evidence located; operational safety evidence remains limited because relevant information is classified |
| 1B.4 Arms control and inspections | Strong evidence: New START extension, U.S. compliance claims, Russian inspection refusal and suspension |
| 1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression | No evidence that Biden used U.S. nuclear forces to shield U.S. aggression; Ukraine policy created an escalation-management question |
| 1B.6 Preventing proliferation | General nonproliferation and export-control framework located; 2023 enforcement outcomes need deeper inspection |
| 1B.7 Independent risk expertise | Evidence of qualified arms-control personnel and expert institutions; independence and dissent require further review |
| 1B.8 Crisis de-escalation | Evidence of deterrence combined with crisis communication and arms-control messaging during Ukraine |
| 1B.9 Other catastrophic risks | Strong evidence of AI-risk executive action and emerging-technology risk diplomacy; implementation outcomes were not yet observable |
| 1B.10 Lasting risk posture | Mixed and unresolved: treaty transparency deteriorated, while risk-reduction institutions and emerging-technology safeguards expanded |

## 3. Evidence index

### WEB-1B-001 — Biden administration extended New START

At the beginning of Biden’s presidency, the United States and Russia extended New START for the full five years allowed by the treaty. The treaty limited deployed strategic nuclear forces and preserved verification and transparency mechanisms.

This was an inherited-period decision with continuing significance in 2023. It directly supports arms control, inspection, and longer-term risk-reduction lenses.

Strongest source: Mallory Stewart, Assistant Secretary of State for Arms Control, Verification and Compliance, remarks at a Brookings event, February 27, 2023. The transcript states that the administration extended New START for the full five years because both sides viewed it as in their security interests. [Brookings transcript](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/fp_20230227_russia_new_start_transcript.pdf), pp. 3–4.

Attribution: strong. The extension was an executive diplomatic decision within the president’s authority.

Contrary evidence: Extension preserved a bilateral framework but did not produce a replacement treaty or prevent subsequent deterioration.

Source limitations: The speaker was a senior U.S. official defending administration policy. The event transcript is useful for the formal history but not independent evaluation.

Independent corroboration: Axios reporting and CRS/FAS materials on New START.

### WEB-1B-002 — Russia refused inspections and suspended New START

During 2022–2023, Russia refused to facilitate treaty inspections and did not convene the treaty’s Bilateral Consultative Commission on schedule. On February 21, 2023, Vladimir Putin announced that Russia was suspending participation in New START.

The U.S. State Department stated that the United States remained within the treaty’s numerical limits and had attempted to preserve Russian inspection rights. The loss of inspection and transparency mechanisms increased uncertainty and the risk of miscalculation.

Target period: 2023, with noncompliance beginning in 2022.

Attribution: direct for the U.S. response; indirect for the Russian decision. Biden’s support for Ukraine and broader confrontation with Russia formed part of the strategic context, but Russia independently chose suspension.

Strongest source: Brookings transcript, including the State Department account of Russian inspection refusal, U.S. readiness to host inspections, and the February 2023 suspension. [Brookings transcript](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/fp_20230227_russia_new_start_transcript.pdf), pp. 3–6.

Independent corroboration: [Axios, February 21, 2023](https://www.axios.com/2023/02/21/putin-russia-ukraine-war-address-new-start-nuclear); Brookings discussion opening remarks; reporting by CBS News and TIME in the supplied catalogue.

Contrary evidence: Russia claimed U.S. noncompliance and cited the changed security environment. The U.S. government rejected those claims.

Cautions: The detailed allegations about inspection access are primarily presented by the U.S. side. Russian legal and operational claims require separate primary-source inspection.

### WEB-1B-003 — Biden responded to New START deterioration by defending arms control

The administration argued that arms control was especially important during periods of heightened tension because it reduces uncertainty, miscalculation, and arms-race incentives. State Department officials said the United States remained prepared to pursue arms-control measures despite Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and treaty suspension.

This supports responsible rhetoric and crisis-de-escalation lenses, while also showing the administration’s attempt to preserve a risk-reduction posture under adverse conditions.

Strongest source: Assistant Secretary Mallory Stewart’s February 2023 Brookings remarks. [Brookings transcript](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/fp_20230227_russia_new_start_transcript.pdf), pp. 2, 5–8.

Attribution: strong for official policy and messaging.

Complications: The same remarks reaffirmed U.S. military readiness and continued support for Ukraine. Arms-control rhetoric therefore coexisted with strategic competition and military assistance.

Source limitations: Official remarks are advocacy material and do not establish that risk actually declined.

### WEB-1B-004 — Ukraine policy created nuclear escalation risk but included restraint measures

The Biden administration continued extensive military, economic, and humanitarian assistance to Ukraine while avoiding direct U.S.–Russia war. Biden and senior officials rejected Russian nuclear coercion, but also maintained that the United States would defend itself and its allies and continue supporting Ukraine.

This is central to 1B.5 and 1B.8. The policy did not use U.S. nuclear weapons to shield aggression, but it operated within a conflict in which Russia used nuclear threats and where escalation risk was repeatedly discussed.

Strongest source: Brookings transcript, which records the State Department’s position that Russian nuclear threats would not stop U.S. support for Ukraine, alongside calls for crisis communication and arms-control stability. [Brookings transcript](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/fp_20230227_russia_new_start_transcript.pdf), pp. 2–8.

Independent corroboration: [State Department Ukraine coalition material](https://stories.state.gov/g7-ukraine/index.html); [CSIS Russia nuclear-rhetoric analysis](https://features.csis.org/deter-and-divide-russia-nuclear-rhetoric-escalation/index.html); [TIME analysis of nuclear risk](https://time.com/6244330/russia-ukraine-war-nuclear-threat).

Contrary evidence: Critics argued that continued weapons transfers and maximalist public positions could increase escalation risk. Supporters argued that deterrence and allied unity reduced the chance of Russian coercion succeeding.

Cautions: The causal effect of U.S. support on nuclear escalation cannot be isolated from Russian military decisions, Ukrainian operations, allied policy, and battlefield developments.

### WEB-1B-005 — The administration maintained nuclear deterrence and modernization

The Biden administration retained the U.S. nuclear triad and modernization framework while reviewing nuclear policy through the 2022 Nuclear Posture Review. The supplied FAS and CRS materials describe continued modernization and debates over force structure, deterrence, and arms-control tradeoffs.

This supports 1B.1, 1B.3, and 1B.10. Modernization can be presented as safety and deterrence policy, but it also preserves large destructive capacity and may contribute to arms competition.

Strongest sources: Federation of American Scientists, *The Biden Administration’s Nuclear Posture Review*; CRS/FAS, *2022 Nuclear Posture Review: Selected Programmatic Issues*. [FAS Nuclear Posture Review analysis](https://fas.org/publication/the-biden-administrations-nuclear-posture-review); [CRS/FAS brief](https://sgp.fas.org/crs/nuke/IF12357.pdf).

Period: broader Biden-period policy with direct relevance to 2023 implementation.

Attribution: strong for administration policy; weaker for operational outcomes.

Contrary evidence: The Congressional Commission on the Strategic Posture of the United States argued in 2023 for a broader nuclear buildup in response to China and Russia. [Strategic Posture Commission report](https://fas.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/Strategic-Posture-Committee-Report-Final.pdf)

Cautions: Force posture and readiness details are partly classified. FAS analysis is technically informed but not itself an official operational audit.

### WEB-1B-006 — The 2023 Strategic Posture Commission called for expanded nuclear capability

The bipartisan Congressional Commission on the Strategic Posture of the United States issued a 2023 report warning that the United States faced a more demanding strategic environment involving Russia and China. It recommended substantial changes, including expanded U.S. nuclear and conventional capabilities.

This is important contrary evidence against treating the Biden-era posture as unambiguously risk-reducing. The report shows that the administration operated within a strategic system pressing toward greater capability and deterrence investment.

Strongest source: Congressional Commission on the Strategic Posture of the United States, final report, 2023. [Full report](https://fas.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/Strategic-Posture-Committee-Report-Final.pdf)

Period: 2023.

Attribution: indirect. The commission was congressional and independent of the White House, though its recommendations affected the policy environment.

Complications: The report was advisory rather than a presidential directive. Its recommendations were contested and should not be treated as implemented Biden policy.

Cautions: The commission’s mandate emphasized strategic sufficiency and deterrence, not a neutral assessment of existential-risk minimization.

### WEB-1B-007 — The administration supported nonproliferation and NPT institutions

The Biden administration continued to present the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty as a central framework for preventing proliferation, supporting peaceful nuclear cooperation, and pursuing disarmament. State Department materials emphasized U.S. treaty commitments and nonproliferation diplomacy.

This supports 1B.4 and 1B.6. The record shows formal commitment, but the available material does not yet establish the full effectiveness of enforcement against illicit procurement networks, allies, firms, or dual-use transfers during 2023.

Strongest sources: U.S. Department of State, *Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty*; remarks by senior U.S. officials at the NPT Review Conference. [NPT policy page](https://2021-2025.state.gov/nuclear-non-proliferation-treaty); [NPT briefing remarks](https://2021-2025.state.gov/remarks-at-the-high-level-briefing-on-u-s-nuclear-policy-at-the-tenth-review-conference-on-the-treaty-of-non-proliferation-of-nuclear-weapons)

Period: broader policy with continuing 2023 relevance.

Attribution: strong for formal diplomatic position.

Cautions: The State Department pages were technically unstable during inspection, so the catalogue’s preserved URLs should be re-opened and page-level excerpts captured before final scoring.

Independent corroboration: UNODA treaty records, IAEA safeguards reporting, SIPRI, FAS, and CRS nonproliferation materials.

### WEB-1B-008 — Senior arms-control personnel had relevant qualifications

Mallory Stewart served as Assistant Secretary of State for Arms Control, Verification and Compliance. The Brookings transcript records that she had previously served as a Biden National Security Council senior director for arms control, disarmament, and nonproliferation, and had prior State Department, legal, and national-laboratory experience.

This supports 1B.7. It is evidence that the administration staffed a key arms-control position with a technically qualified specialist rather than relying solely on political loyalty.

Strongest source: Brookings transcript, biographical introduction, pp. 1–2. [Brookings transcript](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/fp_20230227_russia_new_start_transcript.pdf)

Period: appointment and service spanning 2021–2023.

Attribution: strong for personnel selection; limited for the official’s practical independence or access to dissent.

Complications: A qualified appointment does not prove that expert warnings controlled policy decisions.

Independent corroboration: State Department biography and appointment records should be added.

### WEB-1B-009 — The administration established an AI-risk governance framework

On October 30, 2023, Biden issued Executive Order 14110 on safe, secure, and trustworthy artificial intelligence. The order addressed safety testing, security, civil rights, consumer protection, workers, privacy, and international cooperation. The accompanying OMB analysis described agency responsibilities and anticipated no measurable federal budget impact over five years.

This is the strongest 2023 evidence for 1B.9. It shows formal action against a potentially catastrophic dual-use risk, though implementation and enforcement outcomes were not yet observable by year-end.

Strongest source: OMB, *Budgetary Impact Analysis for Executive Order Entitled “Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence,”* October 30, 2023. [OMB analysis](https://whitehouse.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/AI-EO-BIS.pdf)

Independent corroboration: [Archived Executive Order text](https://digitalcommons.unl.edu/scholcom/263); AP reporting on the order; academic work on AI and biological misuse, including [arXiv:2306.13952](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.13952).

Contrary evidence: The order relied heavily on executive-agency implementation and could be altered by future administrations. Academic and policy analyses warned that voluntary or fragmented controls might not match the scale of the risk.

Cautions: OMB’s analysis describes intended benefits, not demonstrated results. The executive order did not itself create a comprehensive statutory AI-safety regime.

### WEB-1B-010 — U.S. officials identified AI, cyber, space, quantum, and biological risks as strategic-stability issues

In February 2023, the State Department’s arms-control leadership identified AI, autonomous military systems, quantum computing, geoengineering, deepfakes, space debris, and biological misuse as technologies with potential strategic effects. The administration supported international norms, verification work, and responsible military-AI principles.

This expands the chapter beyond nuclear weapons and supports 1B.1, 1B.7, and 1B.9.

Strongest source: Brookings transcript, pp. 6–8, including discussion of AI military use, space-security norms, verification, IPNDV, and CEND. [Brookings transcript](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/fp_20230227_russia_new_start_transcript.pdf)

Attribution: strong for official diplomatic and institutional priorities.

Complications: Norm-building is not equivalent to binding controls, and the transcript does not show measurable reductions in catastrophic risk.

Independent corroboration: academic AI/biological-risk research in the supplied catalogue; Executive Order 14110; UN General Assembly materials on destructive ASAT testing.

Cautions: The speaker’s remit was arms control and diplomacy, not the full federal biosecurity or cyber apparatus.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The United States provided unusually extensive public documentation, but the chapter remains difficult because:

- nuclear force size, alert status, targeting, command systems, and safety incidents are partly classified;
- official accounts of treaty compliance are disputed by Russia;
- nuclear modernization figures mix appropriations, projections, and actual execution;
- crisis escalation cannot be observed directly and requires inference from public behavior;
- the president shares authority with Congress, military institutions, intelligence agencies, allies, and treaty partners;
- official statements reliably document policy positions but may overstate effectiveness;
- FAS, CSIS, Brookings, SIPRI, and CRS provide valuable technical interpretation but differ in institutional perspective;
- AI and biological-risk policy was new in 2023, so implementation evidence was immature;
- later reports, including the 2023 Strategic Posture Commission, describe the strategic environment but should not be confused with Biden administration decisions.

## 6. Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Full 2022 Nuclear Posture Review and classified/unclassified implementation documents.
- Department of Defense budget execution for nuclear modernization and command-and-control programs in FY2023.
- NNSA FY2023 budget and stockpile-stewardship performance reports.
- IAEA safeguards and nonproliferation reporting for U.S.-linked proliferation cases in 2023.
- UNODA and NPT Review Conference records, including U.S. proposals and voting behavior.
- State Department and Russian treaty records concerning New START inspection disputes.
- Congressional hearings on nuclear modernization, arms control, and AI-enabled weapons.
- Federal Register implementation memoranda for Executive Order 14110.
- U.S. biological-security and cyber-risk strategies issued or implemented in 2023.
- Independent assessments of whether Ukraine-related U.S. signaling reduced or increased nuclear escalation risk.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# Chapter 2B — International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security

Joe Biden, United States, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Biden was President and commander-in-chief throughout 2023. He could authorize military assistance, conduct diplomacy, direct executive agencies, use presidential drawdown authority, and veto or sign legislation. Congress controlled appropriations and war-related statutory authorities; courts, allies, Ukraine, Israel, Russia, and regional governments substantially constrained outcomes.

The conflict-exposure gate is clearly met. The United States was supporting Ukraine against Russia’s invasion and became deeply involved in the Israel–Gaza war after October 7 through arms, diplomacy, humanitarian assistance, and Security Council action. Afghanistan was an inherited conflict and withdrawal issue rather than an active 2023 war.

The evidence is strong for formal decisions, arms transfers, public justifications, U.S. diplomatic positions, and UN actions. It is weaker for classified targeting, private diplomatic bargaining, civilian-harm assessments, and the degree of U.S. control over partner conduct.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Evidence status |
|---|---|
| 2B.1 Peaceful alternatives | Mixed: diplomacy and humanitarian negotiations were pursued, but military support remained central in Ukraine and Israel |
| 2B.2 Starting or prolonging war | No evidence Biden initiated the Russian invasion or October 7 conflict; U.S. support may have prolonged Ukraine’s capacity to resist, which is not equivalent to unlawful prolongation |
| 2B.3 Honest justification for force | Administration stated defensive and humanitarian rationales; cluster-munitions decision was unusually explicit about tradeoffs |
| 2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection | Formal safeguards and demining commitments existed; serious concerns remained over cluster munitions and Gaza civilian harm |
| 2B.5 Control of proxies and arms | Strong evidence of arms-recipient conditions for Ukraine; Gaza arms-transfer oversight requires deeper inspection |
| 2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims | Congressional, judicial, media, UN, and NGO scrutiny remained available, though official secrecy and partisan conflict complicated review |
| 2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements | Humanitarian pauses and hostage arrangements were pursued in Gaza; no durable Ukraine settlement was achieved |
| 2B.8 Responsible military resources | Large Ukraine security-assistance commitments addressed a genuine defensive conflict but raised proportionality and stockpile concerns |
| 2B.9 Accountability and remedy | Some oversight and reporting existed; evidence of completed remedies for partner-caused civilian harm is limited |
| 2B.10 Lasting international peace | Mixed and unresolved: alliance cohesion and humanitarian diplomacy improved, while wars continued and arms-control/security tensions worsened |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-2B-001 — U.S. security assistance supported Ukraine’s defensive war

On July 7, 2023, the Department of Defense announced a presidential drawdown package for Ukraine containing air-defense systems, artillery, armored vehicles, anti-armor weapons, mine-clearing equipment, and DPICM cluster munitions. The package was described as supporting Ukraine’s defense against Russia’s continuing war.

The decision directly shaped the military balance and U.S. involvement in the war. It was framed by the administration as assistance to a state defending its territory, not U.S. initiation of hostilities.

Strongest source: U.S. Department of Defense, “Biden Administration Announces Additional Security Assistance for Ukraine,” July 7, 2023. [Defense Department release](https://www.defense.gov/News/Releases/Release/Article/3451570/biden-administration-announces-additional-security-assistance-for-ukraine/)

Locator: package description and list of weapons.

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong. The package was authorized under presidential drawdown authority.

Complications: Continued assistance increased Ukraine’s capacity to resist but also prolonged the battlefield and increased escalation exposure. That does not by itself establish unnecessary prolongation or unlawful conduct.

Cautions: The Defense Department describes U.S. policy and does not independently assess battlefield necessity or civilian consequences.

Independent support: Defense Department July 7 briefing; later Defense Department announcements documenting the scale of assistance.

### WEB-2B-002 — Biden authorized cluster-munitions transfers despite recognized civilian risk

The administration transferred DPICM cluster munitions to Ukraine in July 2023. Biden publicly described the decision as difficult. The administration argued that Ukraine needed artillery, that the munitions had lower dud rates than Russian examples, and that Ukraine had provided assurances about responsible use and recording locations for later demining.

This is the clearest adverse and contested record for civilian protection, lawful arms transfers, and partner control.

Strongest source: UN Special Rapporteur on torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment, communication to the United States, July 14, 2023. The communication reproduces the U.S. rationale and records the Special Rapporteur’s objections. [UN Special Procedures communication](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TMResultsBase/DownLoadPublicCommunicationFile?gId=28244), pp. 1–4.

Independent corroboration: [Defense Department briefing](https://www.defense.gov/News/Transcripts/Transcript/Article/3452000/under-secretary-of-defense-for-policy-dr-colin-kahl-holds-press-briefing/); [Defense Department release](https://www.defense.gov/News/Releases/Release/Article/3451570/biden-administration-announces-additional-security-assistance-for-ukraine/); Amnesty International and Harvard Human Rights Clinic materials in the candidate catalogue.

Contrary evidence: The administration argued that Russia had already used cluster munitions extensively, that Ukraine needed artillery for defense, that the supplied rounds had a dud rate below 2.35%, and that Ukrainian use would avoid civilian-populated urban environments.

Cautions: The UN communication was an urgent appeal, not a court judgment. It relied partly on information supplied by the U.S. government and did not make a final legal determination.

### WEB-2B-003 — The administration attached operational safeguards to cluster-munitions assistance

The Defense Department stated that Ukraine had provided written assurances that DPICM rounds would not be used in civilian-populated urban environments, that locations would be recorded, and that Ukraine would support post-conflict demining. The United States reported more than $95 million already invested in demining activities.

This supports the existence of an arms-recipient-control framework, but not necessarily successful implementation.

Strongest source: Under Secretary of Defense for Policy Colin Kahl, July 7, 2023 press briefing. [Defense Department transcript](https://www.defense.gov/News/Transcripts/Transcript/Article/3452000/under-secretary-of-defense-for-policy-dr-colin-kahl-holds-press-briefing/)

Locator: passages on Ukrainian assurances, dud rates, urban use, location recording, and demining.

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for U.S. conditions and resource commitments; weaker for actual Ukrainian compliance.

Complications: Monitoring was dependent on Ukrainian reporting during active combat. Long-term unexploded-ordnance harm could not be evaluated in 2023.

Cautions: The briefing is an official account and does not independently verify Ukrainian assurances.

### WEB-2B-004 — The UN Special Rapporteur warned that the transfer could violate civilian-protection obligations

The UN Special Rapporteur argued that cluster munitions pose foreseeable risks of indiscriminate injury, unexploded ordnance, displacement, and long-term humanitarian harm. The communication urged the United States to reconsider the transfer and noted possible obligations relating to aid or assistance to internationally wrongful acts.

This provides credible contrary evidence to the administration’s necessity and proportionality rationale.

Strongest source: UN Special Procedures communication, July 14, 2023. [Communication](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TMResultsBase/DownLoadPublicCommunicationFile?gId=28244), pp. 2–5.

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for the fact that the transfer generated formal international legal criticism; not a final finding of U.S. illegality.

Complications: The communication acknowledged that the United States, Ukraine, and Russia were not parties to the Convention on Cluster Munitions. It therefore focused on broader international humanitarian and human-rights obligations.

Cautions: Special-procedures communications are preliminary accountability tools, not binding judgments.

Independent support: ICRC and UNIDIR materials cited in the communication; Amnesty International and Harvard Human Rights Clinic statements.

### WEB-2B-005 — U.S. policy sought to avoid direct war with Russia while maintaining support for Ukraine

The administration continued military support for Ukraine while avoiding direct U.S.–Russia combat. Official statements emphasized that Russia could end the conflict by withdrawing and that U.S. support was intended to enable Ukraine’s defense.

This supports a distinction between supporting a victim of aggression and initiating a war of choice. It also shows the central strategic tradeoff: deterrence and defense versus escalation and conflict duration.

Strongest source: Defense Department August 14, 2023 announcement of additional assistance. [Defense Department announcement](https://www.defense.gov/News/News-Stories/Article/Article/3492273/pentagon-announces-200m-in-security-assistance-for-ukraine/)

Locator: statement that the assistance supported Ukraine in countering Russia’s war and that the United States was not directly entering the conflict.

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for U.S. policy; indirect for battlefield outcomes.

Complications: The United States became a major military supporter and increased weapons production, making it an important party to the conflict environment even without deploying combat forces against Russia.

Cautions: Official language places responsibility entirely on Russia and may understate escalation risks associated with U.S. policy.

### WEB-2B-006 — Gaza diplomacy combined support for Israel with humanitarian pauses

After October 7, Biden supported Israel’s right to respond to Hamas while the administration also pursued hostage negotiations, humanitarian access, and regional diplomacy. The UN Security Council adopted Resolution 2712 on November 15, with the United States abstaining, calling for urgent humanitarian pauses, corridors, hostage release, and compliance with international humanitarian law. Resolution 2720 followed on December 22.

Strongest sources: UN Security Council coverage of Resolution 2712 and the text of Resolution 2720. [Resolution 2712 coverage](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15496.doc.htm), [Resolution 2720 text](https://www.un.org/unispal/document/security-council-resolution-s-res-2720-22dec2023/)

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for U.S. diplomatic posture and vote; limited for Israeli implementation.

Complications: The United States vetoed a Brazilian draft humanitarian-pause resolution on October 18, before later abstaining on Resolution 2712. This indicates changing diplomacy but also an earlier willingness to block Security Council action.

Cautions: UN records establish votes and resolution language, not the full content of private U.S. pressure on Israel or Hamas.

Independent support: State Department travel records; AP reporting in the candidate catalogue; UN Secretary-General reports on implementation.

### WEB-2B-007 — The United States vetoed an October humanitarian-pause resolution

On October 18, 2023, the United States vetoed a Brazilian Security Council draft that would have called for humanitarian pauses, civilian protection, hostage release, and humanitarian access. The United States objected in part to the draft’s failure to condemn Hamas’s attack, while supporting Israel’s right to self-defense.

This is important contrary evidence for peaceful alternatives, civilian protection, and lawful-security diplomacy. It shows that U.S. policy did not consistently prioritize an immediate humanitarian pause at the start of the Gaza war.

Strongest source: UN Security Council, “Security Council Fails to Adopt Resolution Calling for Humanitarian Pauses,” October 18, 2023. [UN record](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15450.doc.htm)

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong. The United States exercised its Security Council veto.

Complications: The proposed text condemned violence and terrorism but, according to the UN record, did not fully reflect the U.S. preference for condemning Hamas and affirming Israel’s right to self-defense.

Cautions: A Security Council vote is only one part of diplomacy. It does not reveal whether private U.S. engagement sought equivalent humanitarian outcomes.

Independent support: UN records on subsequent Resolution 2712; reporting by AP and State Department records.

### WEB-2B-008 — The administration later supported a UN humanitarian-pause resolution

On November 15, 2023, the United States abstained rather than vetoing Resolution 2712. The resolution called for urgent and extended humanitarian pauses, humanitarian corridors, immediate hostage release, and compliance with international humanitarian law. A later Resolution 2720 called for implementation, aid access, and protection of humanitarian personnel.

This indicates movement toward a more humanitarian diplomatic position, though it did not produce a durable ceasefire or end civilian harm.

Strongest source: UN Security Council press release. [Resolution 2712](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15496.doc.htm)

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for U.S. vote and diplomacy; limited for implementation.

Complications: The UN Secretary-General later reported that aid delivery remained severely constrained by bombardment, movement restrictions, fuel shortages, and communications disruptions. [Secretary-General remarks](https://www.un.org/unispal/document/secretary-generals-remarks-to-the-security-council-on-the-implementation-of-res-2712-2023/)

Cautions: Adoption of a resolution does not establish compliance by Israel, Hamas, or other actors.

### WEB-2B-009 — The administration pursued hostage and humanitarian diplomacy through regional partners

Secretary of State Antony Blinken’s 2023 travel record shows intensive regional diplomacy involving Israel, Jordan, Bahrain, Qatar, the United Arab Emirates, and Saudi Arabia after October 7. These contacts created channels for hostage negotiations, humanitarian access, and regional de-escalation.

Strongest source: U.S. Department of State, Office of the Historian, Blinken travel record. [State Department travel record](https://history.state.gov/departmenthistory/travels/secretary/blinken-antony-j)

Locator: October 2023 entries for Israel and regional states.

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for U.S. diplomatic activity.

Complications: The record establishes meetings and travel, not the results of private negotiations. It also does not show that diplomacy prevented broader regional escalation.

Cautions: Official travel records are administrative documentation and omit sensitive details.

Independent support: UN Resolution 2712 and Resolution 2720; AP reporting on hostage and humanitarian negotiations.

### WEB-2B-010 — The United States maintained large military resources for Ukraine

By August 2023, the Defense Department reported more than $43 billion in U.S. security assistance to Ukraine since Russia’s full-scale invasion, including air-defense systems, anti-armor systems, artillery, and other equipment. The administration also increased production of artillery rounds.

This supports the military-resource lens. The spending and procurement addressed an active interstate war, but the scale created questions about proportionality, stockpile depletion, dependence on defense contractors, and the duration of the conflict.

Strongest source: Defense Department, “Pentagon Announces $200M in Security Assistance for Ukraine,” August 14, 2023. [Defense Department announcement](https://www.defense.gov/News/News-Stories/Article/Article/3492273/pentagon-announces-200m-in-security-assistance-for-ukraine/)

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for U.S. resource decisions.

Complications: U.S. assistance was provided through presidential drawdown and Ukraine Security Assistance Initiative authorities, with Congress controlling much of the underlying funding.

Cautions: Defense Department totals may include committed rather than fully delivered assistance, and they do not measure battlefield effectiveness or civilian consequences.

Independent support: Congressional appropriations and CRS Ukraine-assistance reports.

### WEB-2B-011 — Afghanistan withdrawal remained a later accountability issue

The 2023 period did not involve an active U.S. war in Afghanistan, but oversight of the 2021 withdrawal continued. The administration and critics disputed responsibility between Biden’s decisions and conditions inherited from the prior administration. Inspector-general materials and later reporting are relevant to accountability and remedy but should not be treated as a direct 2023 conflict event.

Strongest catalogue leads: USAID Office of Inspector General evaluation and testimony; TIME reporting. These URLs were not successfully opened during this pass.

Period: inherited condition and later retrospective evidence.

Attribution: mixed. Biden authorized the withdrawal, but planning and implementation involved multiple agencies and inherited agreements.

Cautions: This evidence should remain separate from 2023 Ukraine and Gaza findings.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The United States had extensive public records, congressional oversight, independent journalism, UN reporting, and legal institutions. Nevertheless:

- military targeting, intelligence, private diplomacy, and arms-transfer monitoring were partly classified;
- U.S. and partner governments framed events through competing legal and moral narratives;
- official claims about civilian protection often relied on assurances from supported governments;
- Ukraine and Gaza data were collected in active war zones and were contested;
- U.S. legal responsibility for partner conduct is distinct from political responsibility or moral influence;
- adoption of a UN resolution did not guarantee implementation;
- casualty and damage estimates were often preliminary;
- the Afghanistan record was retrospective and politically polarized;
- the U.S. government’s position that support was defensive required independent testing rather than acceptance at face value.

## 5. Important questions for deeper research

1. What monitoring did the United States actually conduct over Ukrainian use of DPICM, and were any violations documented?
2. What civilian-harm assessments preceded the cluster-munitions decision?
3. How much private pressure did Biden apply to Israel concerning targeting, humanitarian access, and proportionality?
4. Did U.S. arms transfers to Israel in late 2023 include conditions, suspension mechanisms, or end-use investigations?
5. What measurable effects did U.S. diplomacy have on the November hostage arrangement and humanitarian pauses?
6. Did U.S. Ukraine policy preserve credible diplomatic alternatives, or did it make a negotiated settlement less likely?
7. What did congressional and inspector-general reviews establish about Afghanistan withdrawal planning and accountability?
8. What portion of Ukraine assistance was delivered, expended, or contractually committed by December 2023?
9. Were civilian casualty and international-law assessments independently reviewed inside the executive branch?

## 7. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full U.S. Conventional Arms Transfer Policy and 2023 implementation records.
- Congressional Research Service analyses of Ukraine assistance and cluster-munitions authorities.
- U.S. Department of State and Defense Department end-use monitoring records.
- UN Commission of Inquiry on Ukraine civilian-harm findings.
- UN reports on implementation of Resolutions 2712 and 2720.
- Congressional and USAID Inspector General investigations of Afghanistan withdrawal.
- U.S. arms-transfer and civilian-harm records concerning Israel and Gaza.
- PA-X and UCDP conflict-event data for 2023.
- Financial execution data for Ukraine security assistance and weapons replenishment.
- Independent legal analyses of U.S. responsibility for aiding partner violations.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Chapter 3B — Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection

Joe Biden, United States, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Biden was President and head of the federal executive throughout 2023. He controlled the Department of Justice, FBI, ATF, DHS, ICE, federal prisons, federal civil-rights enforcement, federal grants, and federal firearms regulation. Most policing, prosecution, prisons, protest regulation, and public safety remained under state, local, and tribal authority. That division sharply limits direct presidential attribution.

The physical-safety nexus is present through homicide, firearm violence, immigration detention, domestic violent extremism, federal civil-rights investigations, and policing. The national homicide rate declined in 2023, but severe racial disparities remained. Federal agencies pursued firearms enforcement and local police investigations, while GAO identified unresolved weaknesses in immigration-detention oversight. The evidence does not support treating all nationwide crime or police violence as Biden’s direct responsibility.

Information quality was comparatively strong, but national crime data were revised and delayed, local police data were incomplete, and nongovernmental databases used different definitions. Federal reports document policy and oversight; they do not by themselves establish that national safety outcomes were caused by the president.

## 2. Lens disposition

| Lens | Evidence status |
|---|---|
| 3B.1 Protection from state abuse | Mixed: federal civil-rights institutions remained active; detention oversight and local police abuses remained concerns |
| 3B.2 Control of coercive forces | Partial federal control; DOJ pattern-or-practice investigations show intervention, but state/local autonomy limits attribution |
| 3B.3 Incitement and targeted hatred | No evidence in inspected material of Biden personally directing violence; polarization and extremist threats remained broader environmental risks |
| 3B.4 Complaints and independent oversight | Strong formal oversight capacity; GAO found ICE inspection and management weaknesses |
| 3B.5 Emergency and surveillance powers | Federal security powers continued under legal review; specific 2023 surveillance implementation requires further inspection |
| 3B.6 Safety without state terror | Homicide declined and no federal state-terror pattern was identified; localized police violence remained significant |
| 3B.7 Protection of vulnerable groups | Mixed: federal civil-rights and firearm policies existed, while Black homicide disparities and immigration-detention risks persisted |
| 3B.8 Safe protest and dissent | National civil liberties remained protected institutionally, but protest-policing and state anti-protest laws require deeper review |
| 3B.9 Restrained crisis response | Federal response was generally institutional rather than militarized; detention and border enforcement remained contested |
| 3B.10 Lasting domestic safety | Unresolved: 2023 safety indicators improved in some areas, but structural violence and oversight gaps remained |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-3B-001 — Homicide declined in 2023 but racial disparities remained severe

The Bureau of Justice Statistics estimated a 2023 homicide-victimization rate of 5.9 per 100,000 people, down from 6.7 in 2022. The rate for Black Americans was 21.3 per 100,000, compared with 3.2 for white Americans.

This is important outcome evidence, but it cannot be directly attributed to Biden. Most homicide prevention authority lies with state and local governments, and national trends reflect many factors.

Strongest source: BJS, *Homicide Victimization in the United States, 2023*, published May 2025, based on NIBRS and Supplementary Homicide Reports. [BJS report](https://bjs.ojp.gov/library/publications/homicide-victimization-united-states-2023)

Locator: highlights, lines 116–126.

Period: target-period data, later publication.

Attribution: weak to moderate. Biden could affect federal grants, firearms enforcement, and national policy, but not most local homicide determinants.

Cautions: The report was published later and updated after initial release. Data coverage and reporting systems changed.

Independent corroboration: FBI, CDC mortality statistics, Mapping Police Violence, and local crime data.

### WEB-3B-002 — Federal firearms policy expanded enforcement against unlicensed dealing

Executive Order 14092, issued March 14, 2023, directed the attorney general to clarify who is “engaged in the business” of dealing firearms and therefore must obtain a federal license and conduct background checks. ATF’s eventual final rule was issued in 2024, so the direct 2023 evidence concerns the executive directive and rulemaking process rather than completed implementation.

The policy sought to reduce firearms trafficking and improve tracing and background-check compliance.

Strongest source: ATF, “Justice Department Publishes New Rule to Update Definition of ‘Engaged in the Business’ as a Firearms Dealer,” published 2024 and describing the 2023 executive order and rulemaking. [ATF release](https://www.atf.gov/news/press-releases/justice-department-publishes-new-rule-update-definition-engaged-business-firearms-dealer)

Period: target-period executive action; later implementation.

Attribution: strong for Biden’s executive direction, moderate for eventual enforcement.

Complications: The rule was subject to later litigation and did not produce a measurable 2023 outcome.

Cautions: A later ATF rule cannot be treated as fully implemented in 2023.

Independent corroboration: ATF rulemaking records and the Bipartisan Safer Communities Act.

### WEB-3B-003 — DOJ used federal authority to investigate local police practices

The Justice Department announced a pattern-or-practice investigation into the Memphis Police Department under the Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act of 1994. The statute permits federal investigation of systemic conduct by state or local law-enforcement agencies that violates constitutional or federal rights.

This shows federal willingness to investigate local coercive-force practices, but it also demonstrates the limits of presidential control: the president did not command Memphis police.

Strongest source: DOJ, “Justice Department Announces Pattern or Practice Investigation of the City of Memphis and the Memphis Police Department.” [DOJ announcement](https://www.justice.gov/archives/opa/pr/justice-department-announces-pattern-or-practice-investigation-city-memphis-and-memphis)

Period: target period.

Attribution: strong for federal oversight action; weak for the underlying local conduct.

Complications: A pattern-or-practice investigation is not itself a finding of liability or proof that remedies succeeded.

Cautions: DOJ announcements describe investigative authority and allegations, not final outcomes.

Independent corroboration: DOJ Civil Rights Division findings, court consent decrees, local records, and independent police-violence databases.

### WEB-3B-004 — Immigration detention oversight had material safety and management weaknesses

GAO reported in January 2023 that ICE detained tens of thousands of noncitizens annually and that ICE could improve oversight, inspection analysis, medical-consent procedures, and detention-facility management. GAO specifically found that ICE did not analyze inspection findings sufficiently to identify systemic trends and that several recommendations remained unaddressed.

This is direct evidence relevant to protection from state abuse, vulnerable groups, complaints, and crisis response.

Strongest source: GAO, *Immigration Detention: ICE Can Improve Oversight and Management*, January 9, 2023. [GAO report](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-23-106350)

Locator: Fast Facts and “What GAO Found,” lines 8–29.

Period: target period, with prior findings carried into 2023.

Attribution: moderate to strong. ICE was a federal agency under the executive branch, although detention was implemented through contractors and facilities across the country.

Complications: GAO identified management weaknesses, not necessarily widespread torture or systematic unlawful killing.

Cautions: The report summarized earlier work and recommendations; it was not a complete 2023 inspection of all facilities.

Independent corroboration: ICE detainee-death reports, DHS civil-rights reports, DHS detention ombudsman reports, and AP investigations.

### WEB-3B-005 — National civil-liberties institutions remained active but structural weaknesses persisted

Freedom House rated the United States “Free” in its 2023 report, while noting strong protections for expression, religion, and political participation alongside polarization, criminal-justice dysfunction, immigration concerns, and disparities in political influence.

This supports the existence of institutional protections and independent scrutiny, but it is broader than physical safety and should not be used as a direct Biden score.

Strongest source: Freedom House, *United States: Freedom in the World 2023*. [Freedom House report](https://freedomhouse.org/country/united-states/freedom-world/2023)

Locator: overview, lines 29–37.

Period: report published for 2023, relying partly on 2022 developments.

Attribution: weak to moderate. National institutional conditions were only partly controlled by Biden.

Complications: The report focuses on political rights and civil liberties, not primarily physical safety.

Cautions: It should be used as contextual evidence, not as a direct measure of federal restraint.

Independent corroboration: Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, ACLU submissions, and DOJ civil-rights records.

### WEB-3B-006 — Independent datasets document continued police killings, but attribution is decentralized

Mapping Police Violence maintains a national database of people killed by police. The resource is important because official federal data on police killings are incomplete and because state and local agencies retain primary operational control over policing.

The database is relevant to state-abuse and coercive-force lenses, but it cannot establish that Biden ordered, encouraged, tolerated, or could feasibly prevent individual local killings.

Strongest source: Mapping Police Violence database. [Mapping Police Violence](https://mappingpoliceviolence.org/)

Period: ongoing dataset including 2023.

Attribution: weak at the individual-incident level; potentially moderate only for federal policy, funding, training, or documented obstruction of remedies.

Cautions: The database uses independent collection and classification methods that should be compared with official data. It does not by itself establish legal culpability.

Independent corroboration: FBI use-of-force data, CDC mortality data, Fatal Encounters, DOJ investigations, and local records.

### WEB-3B-007 — DOJ’s domestic-violent-extremism strategy was subject to Inspector General review

The DOJ Inspector General released an audit in June 2023 concerning the department’s strategy to address domestic violent extremism. DOJ defines domestic violent extremists as U.S.-based actors seeking political or social goals through unlawful violence without direction from a foreign terrorist organization or foreign power.

This is relevant to protection from non-state violence, restrained crisis response, and independent oversight. The existence of an audit shows scrutiny, but the accessible catalogue result does not yet establish the full findings or whether the strategy reduced violence.

Strongest source: DOJ Office of Inspector General, *Audit of the Department of Justice’s Strategy to Address the Domestic Violent Extremism Threat*, June 6, 2023. [OIG report](https://oig.justice.gov/reports/audit-department-justices-strategy-address-domestic-violent-extremism-threat)

Period: target period.

Attribution: moderate to strong for federal strategy and oversight.

Complications: Domestic violent extremism is difficult to measure, and overbroad categorization could create civil-liberties risks.

Cautions: The full audit findings require extraction before conclusions about effectiveness or abuse.

Independent corroboration: Congressional testimony, FBI threat assessments, court records, and civil-liberties organizations.

### WEB-3B-008 — Federal firearm and violent-crime agencies reported a 2023 decline, but causal attribution is uncertain

ATF leadership later stated that violent-crime and gun-violence rates declined in many cities during 2023 and linked the outcome to federal and local enforcement efforts. This is an official representation rather than an independent causal evaluation.

The claim is useful as administration rhetoric and as a hypothesis for deeper research, but it should not be treated as proof that Biden policy caused the decline.

Strongest source: ATF director’s prepared remarks accompanying the 2024 final rule. [ATF remarks](https://www.atf.gov/sites/default/files2/2026-01/director_finalrule_statement_final_508c.pdf)

Period: retrospective reference to 2023.

Attribution: moderate for agency claims; weak for causal effect.

Complications: City-level declines may reflect local policing, demographic changes, post-pandemic normalization, and reporting differences.

Cautions: The source was published after the target period and had an institutional interest in defending ATF enforcement.

Independent corroboration: BJS, FBI, CDC, and academic crime-trend analyses.

### WEB-3B-009 — Federal oversight did not eliminate the risk of detention-related abuse

GAO’s findings, together with the existence of DHS civil-rights and detention-ombudsman reporting systems, show that detainees had formal complaint and oversight channels. However, unresolved inspection-analysis and medical-care weaknesses meant that the existence of oversight did not guarantee effective prevention or remedy.

This is a composite institutional finding: formal accountability existed, but implementation gaps remained.

Strongest sources: GAO report; DHS Office for Civil Rights and Civil Liberties annual report; DHS Office of the Immigration Detention Ombudsman annual report. [GAO](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-23-106350), [DHS CRCL report](https://www.dhs.gov/publication/2023-annual-report-office-civil-rights-and-civil-liberties), [DHS OIDO report](https://www.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2024-01/2023-oido-annual-report.pdf)

Period: target period and inherited agency conditions.

Attribution: moderate to strong for federal institutional responsibility.

Complications: Contractors, state facilities, federal appropriations, and litigation also shaped detention conditions.

Cautions: DHS annual reports are self-reporting documents and require comparison with GAO, court, NGO, and detainee testimony.

Independent corroboration: ACLU, Amnesty International, Human Rights Watch, AP investigations, and ICE detainee-death records.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The United States offered extensive data, courts, inspectors general, congressional oversight, civil-rights litigation, independent media, and civil-society monitoring. Major limitations were:

- most policing is state and local, not presidential;
- national homicide and police-killing data were compiled through different systems and updated later;
- federal agency reports often describe policy intentions rather than outcomes;
- immigration-detention conditions varied by facility and contractor;
- police-violence databases may differ in inclusion criteria;
- domestic-extremism investigations involve secrecy and classification risks;
- partisan narratives made attribution and threat characterization contentious;
- victims, detainees, and local communities had unequal access to lawyers, journalists, and complaint mechanisms.

## 5. Questions for deeper research

1. What were the complete 2023 findings of DHS civil-rights and detention-ombudsman reports?
2. How many ICE detainee deaths, serious injuries, medical denials, and substantiated complaints occurred in 2023?
3. Did ICE implement GAO recommendations during the year?
4. What did the DOJ domestic-extremism audit find about surveillance, data quality, and civil-liberties safeguards?
5. What federal grants or DOJ programs measurably affected local homicide and firearm violence?
6. Did Biden’s firearm initiatives change prosecutions, background checks, trafficking investigations, or firearm deaths?
7. What were the 2023 federal use-of-force and police-killing data, compared with independent datasets?
8. Which local police investigations produced consent decrees, reforms, discipline, or victim remedies?
9. What evidence exists of federal treatment of protest movements, including environmental, racial-justice, labor, and immigration protests?
10. Were vulnerable groups—Black communities, migrants, children, women, LGBTQ people, and people with disabilities—protected equitably across federal programs?

- DHS Office for Civil Rights and Civil Liberties 2023 annual report.
- DHS Office of the Immigration Detention Ombudsman 2023 report.

- Federal firearm-trafficking prosecutions and ATF 2023 enforcement data.

- Congressional and court records on surveillance authorities and protest-related investigations.
