--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence  
Joe Biden, United States, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Joe Biden was president throughout 2023, with substantial authority over federal agencies, appointments, foreign policy, implementation of enacted laws, and executive regulations. His practical authority was constrained by a Republican House, Democratic Senate, federal courts, state governments, administrative capacity, litigation, and external shocks including the Ukraine war and inflation.

The clearest governing program was “Investing in America”: infrastructure, domestic semiconductor production, clean energy, manufacturing, labor-market investment, and expanded social relief. Evidence shows substantial conversion of this program into agencies, funding mechanisms, grants, tax rules, and early projects. It also shows significant limits: many programs had long implementation horizons, some outcomes were based on projected rather than realized benefits, and courts blocked or constrained major initiatives such as broad student-debt cancellation.

## 2. Evidence records

### WEB-8B-001 — Industrial and infrastructure policy became an administrative program

The Biden administration translated the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act, CHIPS and Science Act, and Inflation Reduction Act into a coordinated “Investing in America” program focused on infrastructure, manufacturing, semiconductors, clean energy, and workforce development.

- Source: “Biden Administration Releases Implementation Strategy for $50 Billion CHIPS for America Program,” National Institute of Standards and Technology, September 7, 2022, [NIST](https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2022/09/biden-administration-releases-implementation-strategy-50-billion-chips).
- Locator: program goals and implementation strategy.
- Status: inherited program with target-period implementation.
- Attribution: strong; Biden signed the legislation and his administration created the implementation machinery.
- Complications: the source is official and describes intended design, not completed outcomes.
- Independent support: Treasury analysis and EDA implementation records.

### WEB-8B-002 — CHIPS implementation created concrete staffing, funding, and guardrails

By 2023, Commerce had launched the first $39 billion CHIPS funding opportunity, received more than 460 company statements of interest across 42 states, established a 140-person implementation team, issued tax-credit guidance, and proposed national-security guardrails.

- Source: “FACT SHEET: One Year after the CHIPS and Science Act,” American Presidency Project reproducing the administration’s fact sheet, August 2023, [fact sheet](https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/fact-sheet-one-year-after-the-chips-and-science-act-biden-harris-administration-marks).
- Locator: lines describing the funding opportunity, staffing, tax-credit rules, and guardrails.
- Status: target-period.
- Attribution: strong for administrative action; outcome attribution remains provisional.
- Complications: the fact sheet is an administration-produced account and reports applications and processes rather than completed factories or final economic effects.
- Independent support: [Axios reported manufacturing-construction and job projections](https://www.axios.com/2023/08/21/manufacturing-job-boom-semiconductor-renewable-energy).

### WEB-8B-003 — Regional and technology investments were implemented through EDA

In 2023, the Economic Development Administration invested approximately $403 million in 584 projects, which grantees estimated would support more than 27,000 jobs and nearly $8.5 billion in private investment. Biden also announced 31 inaugural Tech Hubs and 29 strategy-development grants.

- Source: “EDA 2023: A Year of Impact,” U.S. Economic Development Administration, December 8, 2023, [EDA](https://www.eda.gov/news/blog/2023/12/08/eda-2023-year-impact), lines 95–111.
- Status: target-period.
- Attribution: strong for federal implementation; projected jobs and private investment are not realized-outcome measures.
- Complications: EDA’s account is self-reported and uses grantee estimates.
- Independent support: the Brookings implementation literature identified administrative capacity and local intermediaries as important determinants of whether federal funds become completed projects.

### WEB-8B-004 — Infrastructure funds produced observable early environmental projects

By October 2023, EPA reported that Infrastructure Law resources had assessed 167 brownfield properties, prepared 36 for anticipated reuse, obligated funds for 152 Superfund sites, and directed substantial funding toward environmental-justice communities.

- Source: “EPA Celebrates Two Years of Progress Under President Biden’s Bipartisan Infrastructure Law,” U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, 2023, [EPA](https://www.epa.gov/newsreleases/epa-celebrates-two-years-progress-under-president-bidens-bipartisan-infrastructure-law), lines 22–39.
- Status: target-period.
- Attribution: strong for agency implementation under Biden’s administration.
- Complications: the source reports obligations and project milestones, not final health, employment, or environmental outcomes.
- Independent support: EPA’s statutory funding description and project-level records.

### WEB-8B-005 — Manufacturing investment increased, but causation and durability remain uncertain

Treasury reported that real U.S. manufacturing construction spending had doubled since the end of 2021, linking the increase to the Infrastructure, CHIPS, and Inflation Reduction Acts. Independent reporting similarly described a manufacturing-construction surge but noted labor-supply bottlenecks.

- Source: “Treasury Releases Analysis of the Boom in U.S. Construction of Manufacturing Facilities,” U.S. Department of the Treasury, [Treasury](https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/jy1580), lines 537–538.
- Corroboration: [Axios](https://www.axios.com/2023/08/21/manufacturing-job-boom-semiconductor-renewable-energy), lines 50–61.
- Status: broader ruler-period outcome with 2023 relevance.
- Attribution: moderate; policy contributed, but interest rates, global supply-chain shifts, private investment, and post-pandemic normalization were alternative causes.
- Complications: construction spending is not equivalent to completed production, productivity, or durable employment.

### WEB-8B-006 — The administration managed a major foreign-policy program through coalition coordination

In 2023, the administration coordinated financial and institutional support for Ukraine, including budget support, reconstruction planning, anti-corruption institution-building, sanctions enforcement, and allied coordination.

- Source: “Administration of Joseph R. Biden, Jr., 2023,” Government Publishing Office, 2023, [Presidential Documents](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/DCPD-202300134/pdf/DCPD-202300134.pdf), pp. 1–2, lines 67–96.
- Status: target-period.
- Attribution: strong for U.S. diplomatic commitments and coordination; battlefield outcomes were not fully under Biden’s control.
- Complications: the document records formal commitments and policy positions, not complete implementation or success.
- Independent support: [State Department year-end military-assistance announcement](https://2021-2025.state.gov/biden-administration-announces-additional-u-s-military-assistance-for-ukraine-2/).

### WEB-8B-007 — Student-debt policy showed both adaptation and failure

Biden’s broad student-loan forgiveness plan was blocked while litigation proceeded; the Supreme Court later rejected the administration’s legal authority under the HEROES Act. The administration then shifted toward rulemaking under the Higher Education Act and the SAVE repayment plan.

- Source: “Supreme Court student loan hearing: What you need to know,” Associated Press, February 28, 2023, [AP](https://apnews.com/article/student-loan-forgiveness-scotus-what-to-know-06a8ac6187fb39b2edc2cf5a379c22c0), lines 1744–1774.
- Corroboration: [TIME analysis](https://time.com/6331512/student-loan-forgiveness-hea-negotiated-rulemaking/), lines 84–99.
- Status: target-period.
- Attribution: strong; Biden made the commitment and the Education Department pursued implementation.
- Complications: the Supreme Court’s legal judgment constrained presidential authority. The policy shift demonstrates adaptation, but not achievement of the original goal.

### WEB-8B-008 — Divided government required negotiated implementation

Congress enacted the Fiscal Responsibility Act on June 3, 2023, suspending the debt limit and imposing discretionary-spending caps and other program changes. Biden signed the law after negotiations with congressional leaders.

- Source: [Congress.gov H.R. 3746 summary](https://www.congress.gov/bill/118th-congress/house-bill/3746/summary/00).
- Status: target-period.
- Attribution: strong for Biden’s signature and negotiation role; Congress controlled the statutory outcome.
- Complications: the agreement avoided default but constrained later administrative spending and reflected limited unilateral authority.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 Clear governing program:** Strongly evidenced by the Investing in America agenda and repeated infrastructure, semiconductor, climate, and workforce priorities.
- **8B.2 Turning goals into machinery:** Strongly evidenced by CHIPS staffing, funding opportunities, tax-credit rules, guardrails, and EDA programs.
- **8B.3 Mobilizing the ruling system:** Partially evidenced; federal agencies mobilized, but state and private-sector execution remained essential.
- **8B.4 Capable implementers:** Moderate evidence; agencies created specialized teams, but a systematic personnel and replacement review remains incomplete.
- **8B.5 Coordination and control:** Moderate evidence through cross-agency CHIPS, EPA, EDA, Treasury, and Ukraine coordination; territorial consistency requires deeper study.
- **8B.6 From plans to practice:** Strong early evidence from grants, obligations, implementation teams, and regulatory guidance.
- **8B.7 Progress toward chosen goals:** Mixed; manufacturing investment and project activity moved toward stated goals, while student-debt cancellation failed in its original form.
- **8B.8 Learning and correction:** Moderate evidence from the shift after *Biden v. Nebraska* toward alternative statutory and regulatory routes.
- **8B.9 Managing crises and resistance:** Strong evidence of negotiated debt-limit management and coalition support for Ukraine; the student-loan litigation shows limits imposed by courts.
- **8B.10 Durable goal achievement:** Not yet fully determinable in 2023. Many programs had multi-year implementation horizons, and durability depended on future appropriations, litigation, state execution, and private investment.

## 4. Information limitations

Official sources are strongest for laws, budgets, staffing, grants, and formal milestones but tend to present projected benefits favorably. Independent reporting helps identify labor bottlenecks and legal constraints, but often cannot separate Biden policy effects from interest rates, global supply chains, private investment, or state-level implementation. Many 2023 programs were too new for reliable final outcome evaluation.

## 5. Remaining research questions

1. What share of announced CHIPS, IRA, and infrastructure commitments had actually been obligated, spent, or completed by December 2023?
2. Which projects met timelines, labor standards, domestic-content rules, and environmental requirements?
3. How much of the manufacturing and employment growth was additional rather than displaced or caused by global recovery?
4. Which agencies experienced staffing or procurement bottlenecks?
5. Did the administration systematically revise programs after audits, delays, litigation, or weak uptake?

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted evidence

NIST CHIPS strategy; CHIPS implementation fact sheet; EDA 2023 report; EPA infrastructure report; Treasury manufacturing analysis; Government Publishing Office Ukraine record; AP and TIME student-loan analyses; Congress.gov Fiscal Responsibility Act record.

### Corroborating sources

- [Axios manufacturing analysis](https://www.axios.com/2023/08/21/manufacturing-job-boom-semiconductor-renewable-energy)
- [Brookings implementation research](https://www.brookings.edu/articles/five-roles-communities-need-for-implementing-once-in-a-generation-federal-resources)
- [CRS climate-policy report](https://crsreports.congress.gov/product/pdf/R/R47068)
- [CRS foreign-operations budget analysis](https://www.congress.gov/crs-products/product/pdf/R/R47070)

### Promising leads not fully inspected

The complete CRS climate report; CBO budget execution reports; GAO infrastructure audits; Department of Commerce award notices; Department of Energy project-level records; Department of Education SAVE implementation records; and independent evaluations of Ukraine assistance.

Source count: 8 developed evidence records; 15 candidates inspected or opened; 10 lenses assessed; 5 major implementation gaps remain.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

# Review-round 1 continuation

The continuation added evidence for all eight chapters. The strongest additions concern independent oversight, contrary humanitarian findings, administrative weaknesses, and beneficiary-level outcomes. The remaining gaps are mostly classified, confidential, or multi-year implementation questions.

The prior `research-ledger-manifest.json` could not be read or updated because the workspace execution sandbox failed with `bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR: Operation not permitted`. The new records below use globally unique continuation IDs and preserve exact lens mappings for later manifest insertion.

## 1B — Catastrophic and nuclear risk

### WEB-R1-1B-001 — Independent strategic review identified unresolved nuclear and biological risks

The 2023 Congressional Commission on the Strategic Posture recommended renewed arms-control and risk-reduction measures, stronger nonproliferation enforcement, and multilateral transparency mechanisms for dangerous pathogens and Biological Weapons Convention violations.

Source: *America’s Strategic Posture*, Congressional Commission on the Strategic Posture of the United States, 2023, [FAS-hosted PDF](https://fas.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/Strategic-Posture-Committee-Report-Final.pdf), pp. 99 and 123–124.

This is target-period evidence, but it is a congressional commission’s assessment and recommendations, not proof of Biden-administration failure. It demonstrates that independent experts viewed the inherited posture as exposed to nuclear competition, proliferation, and biosecurity risks. No source reviewed established a 2023 nuclear accident or proliferation-enforcement failure.

### WEB-R1-1B-002 — Section 702 oversight found both security value and serious civil-liberties risks

The Privacy and Civil Liberties Oversight Board’s September 2023 report concluded that Section 702 remained valuable against terrorism, weapons proliferation, and cyber threats, while also creating serious privacy and civil-liberties risks.

Source: PCLOB, *Report on the Surveillance Program Operated Pursuant to Section 702*, September 28, 2023, [PDF](https://secure.brennancenter.org/files/brennan/upload/2023%20PCLOB%20702%20Report.pdf), pp. 26 and 202–210.

This is target-period, independent oversight evidence. It supports the existence of review and correction mechanisms but does not establish that Biden personally caused or remedied every operational defect.

## 2B — International peace and lawful security

### WEB-R1-2B-001 — The cluster-munition transfer created documented civilian-harm and legal-risk concerns

Human Rights Watch reported that Biden’s July 2023 decision to transfer cluster munitions to Ukraine reversed prior U.S. policy and created long-term civilian risks. Congress’s CRS confirmed that the transfer relied on presidential statutory authority, Ukrainian assurances against use in populated urban areas, and promises to record strike locations and conduct demining.

Sources: Human Rights Watch, July 14, 2023, [HRW](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/07/14/us-cluster-munition-transfer-ukraine-ignores-history-of-civilian-harm), lines 630–653; CRS, *Cluster Munitions: Background and Issues for Congress*, 2023, [Congress.gov](https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/RS22907), lines 674–690.

This is target-period evidence strongly attributable to Biden’s formal decision. HRW is an advocacy organization, while CRS records official safeguards and legal authority. No independent 2023 end-use audit proving compliance was located.

### WEB-R1-2B-002 — Independent end-use verification remained incomplete

The reviewed CRS account documents written Ukrainian assurances and location-recording commitments, but not completed independent verification of all uses or civilian-harm outcomes. This is a material research gap rather than evidence of noncompliance.

Source: CRS, *Cluster Munitions*, 2023, [Congress.gov](https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/RS22907).

## 3B — Domestic safety and protection

### WEB-R1-3B-001 — GAO found continuing weaknesses in immigration-detention oversight

GAO reported in January 2023 that ICE could improve the safety, humanity, and management of detention facilities; several recommendations remained unaddressed, including stronger accountability for medical care, cleanliness, and compliance with detention standards.

Source: GAO, *Immigration Detention: ICE Can Improve Oversight and Management*, January 9, 2023, [GAO](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-23-106350), lines 19–28.

This is target-period independent oversight evidence. It is attributable to the federal administration’s detention system, but detention operations also depend on contractors and facility-level management. It does not establish that Biden ordered abusive conduct.

### WEB-R1-3B-002 — Medical-consent oversight had identifiable remedy mechanisms

GAO recommended that ICE require facilities to collect and review informed-consent documentation for off-site medical care. ICE concurred and stated that it would revise its directive and audit tools.

Source: GAO, *Immigration Detention: ICE Needs to Strengthen Oversight of Informed Consent for Medical Care*, [GAO](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-23-105196), recommendations section.

This supports both a documented weakness and an administrative correction pathway. Later implementation confirmation was not fully target-period evidence.

## 4B — Political freedom

### WEB-R1-4B-001 — Independent surveillance oversight preserved public review while documenting serious risks

The PCLOB’s 2023 Section 702 report was publicly released before congressional reauthorization decisions and carried forward recommendations concerning U.S.-person queries, privacy, and judicial review.

Source: PCLOB, September 28, 2023, [report](https://secure.brennancenter.org/files/brennan/upload/2023%20PCLOB%20702%20Report.pdf), pp. 26 and 202–210.

This supports the continued operation of an independent oversight institution and public scrutiny. It also complicates claims of fully restrained surveillance. Classified information limited the completeness of public review.

### WEB-R1-4B-002 — Executive secrecy and surveillance remained partly unresolved

The report was deliberately unclassified “to the greatest extent possible,” meaning that important operational details remained unavailable. This limits conclusions about the full scope of surveillance, violations, and remedies during 2023.

## 5B — Economic well-being

### WEB-R1-5B-001 — Industrial policy required local intermediaries and coordination

TIME’s October 2023 analysis described Bidenomics as an administrative system requiring regional consortia, state and local incentives, and cross-government coordination. It identified seven regional hydrogen hubs selected from 79 proposals.

Source: Rohan Sandhu, “Bottom Up Bidenomics,” TIME, October 16, 2023, [TIME](https://time.com/6324411/bottom-up-bidenomics/), lines 2–4.

This is independent policy analysis supporting the claim that implementation depended on intermediaries rather than presidential direction alone. It also identifies a bottleneck: federal programs could not succeed without local administrative capacity and private participation.

### WEB-R1-5B-002 — Distributional household outcomes require separate measurement

The 2023 Census poverty report provides official poverty and supplemental poverty estimates, while the USDA reported that 5.1% of households experienced very low food security in 2023, statistically unchanged from 2022.

Sources: Census Bureau, *Poverty in the United States: 2023*, [Census](https://www.census.gov/library/publications/2024/demo/p60-283.html); USDA ERS, *Household Food Security in the United States in 2023*, [USDA](https://www.ers.usda.gov/publications/109895), lines 94–99.

These are outcome sources, mostly later-published but measuring 2023. They are not sufficient to attribute household conditions to Biden because inflation, labor markets, state policy, and inherited trends also mattered.

## 6B — Social well-being

### WEB-R1-6B-001 — Medicaid unwinding produced significant coverage disruption

KFF found that about one in five surveyed Medicaid enrollees reported being disenrolled during 2023; 23% of those disenrolled remained uninsured. Among people attempting renewal, 58% experienced at least one problem, commonly long call-center waits.

Source: KFF, *KFF Survey of Medicaid Unwinding*, [KFF](https://www.kff.org/medicaid/kff-survey-of-medicaid-unwinding/), lines 226–255.

This is target-period beneficiary-level evidence. Federal policy shaped the unwinding rules and oversight, but states administered renewals, so attribution to Biden is shared and limited.

### WEB-R1-6B-002 — Federal and state responsibility complicates welfare attribution

KFF’s Medicaid analysis identifies state renewal systems, pandemic-era federal rules, enhanced federal matching funds, and administrative capacity as interacting causes. The evidence therefore supports a mixed finding: federal policy created the framework, but implementation and coverage loss were not solely presidential decisions.

## 7B — Personal integrity

### WEB-R1-7B-001 — Democratic House investigators disputed the Republican account of DOJ interference

A later Democratic Judiciary Committee report argued that the Tax Division did not block David Weiss from bringing charges and that available evidence did not establish obstruction by another U.S. attorney.

Source: House Judiciary Democrats, *Trying to Prove a Negative*, 2025 retrospective, [PDF](https://democrats-judiciary.house.gov/UploadedFiles/Weiss_Report_CMC_1.2.25.pdf), pp. 95–96, lines 3326–3348.

This is later retrospective evidence about 2023 events. It is partisan and should not be treated as dispositive, but it is important contrary evidence to the 2023 House Republican report.

### WEB-R1-7B-002 — The personal-integrity nexus remains unresolved

The competing congressional reports agree that the Hunter Biden investigation and presidential statements were politically consequential, but they disagree about whether DOJ personnel were obstructed or whether Weiss lacked authority. Neither source reviewed established that Joe Biden personally directed prosecutorial decisions, accepted money in exchange for official action, or obstructed an investigation.

## 8B — Effectiveness and competence

### WEB-R1-8B-001 — Implementation depended on multi-level administrative capacity

TIME’s analysis of Bidenomics emphasized regional consortia and coordination among federal, state, local, and private actors. The evidence supports a functioning mobilization strategy but also shows that presidential announcements could not guarantee delivery.

Source: TIME, October 16, 2023, [Bottom Up Bidenomics](https://time.com/6324411/bottom-up-bidenomics/), lines 2–4.

### WEB-R1-8B-002 — Formal implementation outputs exceeded independently verified final outcomes

Across CHIPS, infrastructure, Medicaid, Ukraine assistance, and surveillance, the reviewed evidence is strongest for laws, staffing, grants, oversight, and announced milestones. Independent evidence of completed projects, long-term distributional effects, end-use compliance, and durable outcomes remains thinner.

This is a cross-chapter synthesis, not a separate factual event. It is supported by the GAO, PCLOB, KFF, CRS, and TIME records above.

## Updated gaps and saturation

- **1B:** Nuclear and emerging-risk policy received stronger independent assessment, but nuclear-safety outcomes and cyber/biological implementation remain partly inaccessible.
- **2B:** Cluster-munition civilian-harm concerns and official safeguards were both extracted; independent end-use monitoring remains unresolved.
- **3B:** GAO oversight findings materially improve the detention record; detainee-death and protest-policing evidence remain open.
- **4B:** PCLOB provides a strong independent surveillance record; voting-access outcomes and secrecy remedies remain incomplete.
- **5B:** Household poverty and food-security outcomes were added; industrial-policy causation and distribution remain unresolved.
- **6B:** Beneficiary-level Medicaid disruption was added; child food insecurity, health outcomes, and remedies require further work.
- **7B:** Contrary congressional evidence was added; the personal knowledge, direction, benefit, and obstruction questions remain unresolved.
- **8B:** Independent implementation analysis was added; project completion, durable outcomes, and procurement audits remain open.

No chapter is fully saturated. Further work should remain bounded to the specific gaps above.

## Source candidates inspected

Inspected sources included the Strategic Posture Commission report, Human Rights Watch cluster-munition analysis, CRS cluster-munitions report, GAO detention reports, PCLOB Section 702 report, KFF Medicaid survey, USDA food-security report, Census poverty report, TIME’s Bidenomics analysis, and the Democratic congressional Weiss report. Several additional ICE, CBO, GAO, and project-level sources remain leads.
